</div><div class="mw-content-ltr mw-parser-output" lang="en" dir="ltr"><div class="shortdescription nomobile noexcerpt noprint searchaux" style="display:none">Facts provided or learned about something or someone</div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1320445320">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}@media print{body.ns-0 .mw-parser-output .hatnote{display:none!important}}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">For other uses, see <a href="/wiki/Information_(disambiguation)" class="mw-disambig" title="Information (disambiguation)">Information (disambiguation)</a>.</div>

<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Outline_of_communication" title="Outline of communication">Outline</a></li></ul></td>
</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1333133064" /><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1239400231">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}html.skin-theme-clientpref-night .mw-parser-output .navbar li a abbr{color:var(--color-base)!important}@media(prefers-color-scheme:dark){html.skin-theme-clientpref-os .mw-parser-output .navbar li a abbr{color:var(--color-base)!important}}@media print{.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:none!important}}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Communication" title="Template:Communication"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Communication" title="Template talk:Communication"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a href="/wiki/Special:EditPage/Template:Communication" title="Special:EditPage/Template:Communication"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Information</b> is an <a href="/wiki/Abstraction" title="Abstraction">abstract concept</a> that refers to something which has the power <a href="/wiki/Communication" title="Communication">to inform</a>.  At the most fundamental level, it pertains to the <a href="/wiki/Interpretation_(philosophy)" title="Interpretation (philosophy)">interpretation</a> (perhaps <a href="/wiki/Interpretation_(logic)" title="Interpretation (logic)">formally</a>) of that which may be <a href="/wiki/Sense" title="Sense">sensed</a>, or their <a href="/wiki/Abstraction" title="Abstraction">abstractions</a>.  Any natural process that is not completely <a href="/wiki/Random" class="mw-redirect" title="Random">random</a> and any observable <a href="/wiki/Pattern" title="Pattern">pattern</a> in any <a href="/wiki/Media_(communication)" class="mw-redirect" title="Media (communication)">medium</a> can be said to convey some amount of information.  Whereas <a href="/wiki/Digital_signals" class="mw-redirect" title="Digital signals">digital signals</a> and other <a href="/wiki/Data" title="Data">data</a> use discrete <a href="/wiki/Sign_(semiotics)" title="Sign (semiotics)">signs</a> to convey information, other phenomena and artifacts such as <a href="/wiki/Analog_signals" class="mw-redirect" title="Analog signals">analogue signals</a>, <a href="/wiki/Poems" class="mw-redirect" title="Poems">poems</a>, <a href="/wiki/Picture" class="mw-redirect" title="Picture">pictures</a>, <a href="/wiki/Music" title="Music">music</a> or other <a href="/wiki/Sound" title="Sound">sounds</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Current_(fluid)" title="Current (fluid)">currents</a> convey information in a more continuous form.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Anderson&#95;1-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Anderson-1"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>1<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>  Information is not <a href="/wiki/Knowledge" title="Knowledge">knowledge</a> itself, but the <a href="/wiki/Meaning_(philosophy)" title="Meaning (philosophy)">meaning</a> that may be derived from a <a href="/wiki/Representation_(mathematics)" title="Representation (mathematics)">representation</a> through interpretation.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Yockey&#95;2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Yockey-2"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>2<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>The concept of <i>information</i> is relevant to and connected with various concepts,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Floridi&#95;3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Floridi-3"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>3<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> including <a href="/wiki/Constraint_(information_theory)" title="Constraint (information theory)">constraint</a>, <a href="/wiki/Communication" title="Communication">communication</a>, <a href="/wiki/Control_system" title="Control system">control</a>, <a href="/wiki/Data" title="Data">data</a>, <a href="/wiki/Shape" title="Shape">form</a>, <a href="/wiki/Education" title="Education">education</a>, <a href="/wiki/Knowledge" title="Knowledge">knowledge</a>, <a href="/wiki/Meaning_(linguistics)" class="mw-redirect" title="Meaning (linguistics)">meaning</a>, <a href="/wiki/Understanding" title="Understanding">understanding</a>, <a href="/wiki/Stimulation" title="Stimulation">mental stimuli</a>, <a href="/wiki/Pattern_theory" title="Pattern theory">pattern</a>, <a href="/wiki/Perception" title="Perception">perception</a>, <a href="/wiki/Proposition#Treatment_in_logic" title="Proposition">proposition</a>, <a href="/wiki/Knowledge_representation" class="mw-redirect" title="Knowledge representation">representation</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Entropy_(information_theory)" title="Entropy (information theory)">entropy</a>.
</p><p>Information is often processed iteratively: Data available at one step are <a href="/wiki/Data_processing" title="Data processing">processed</a> into information to be interpreted and processed at the next step.  For example, in <a href="/wiki/Written_text" class="mw-redirect" title="Written text">written text</a> each <a href="/wiki/Symbol" title="Symbol">symbol</a> or <a href="/wiki/Letter_(alphabet)" title="Letter (alphabet)">letter</a> conveys information relevant to the word it is part of, each word conveys information relevant to the phrase it is part of, each phrase conveys information relevant to the sentence it is part of, and so on until at the final step information is interpreted and becomes knowledge in a given <a href="/wiki/Knowledge_domain" class="mw-redirect" title="Knowledge domain">domain</a>.  In a <a href="/wiki/Digital_signal" title="Digital signal">digital signal</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bit" title="Bit">bits</a> may be interpreted into the symbols, letters, numbers, or structures that convey the information available at the next level up.  The key characteristic of information is that it is subject to interpretation and processing.
</p><p>The derivation of information from a signal or message may be thought of as the resolution of <a href="/wiki/Ambiguity" title="Ambiguity">ambiguity</a> or <a href="/wiki/Uncertainty" title="Uncertainty">uncertainty</a> that arises during the interpretation of patterns within the signal or message.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>4<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Information may be structured as <a href="/wiki/Data" title="Data">data</a>. Redundant data can be <a href="/wiki/Data_compression" title="Data compression">compressed</a> up to an optimal size, which is the theoretical limit of compression.
</p><p>The information available through a collection of data may be derived by analysis.  For example, a restaurant collects data from every customer order. That information may be analyzed to produce knowledge that is put to use when the business subsequently wants to identify the most popular or least popular dish.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (May 2024)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Information can be transmitted in time, via <a href="/wiki/Data_storage" title="Data storage">data storage</a>, and space, via <a href="/wiki/Communication" title="Communication">communication</a> and <a href="/wiki/Telecommunication" class="mw-redirect" title="Telecommunication">telecommunication</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Hilbertvideo2011&#95;5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hilbertvideo2011-5"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>5<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Information is expressed either as the content of a <a href="/wiki/Message" title="Message">message</a> or through direct or indirect <a href="/wiki/Observation" title="Observation">observation</a>. That which is <a href="/wiki/Perceived" class="mw-redirect" title="Perceived">perceived</a> can be construed as a message in its own right, and in that sense, all information is always conveyed as the content of a message.
</p><p>Information can be <a href="/wiki/Encoded" class="mw-redirect" title="Encoded">encoded</a> into various forms for <a href="/wiki/Transmission_(telecommunications)" class="mw-redirect" title="Transmission (telecommunications)">transmission</a> and <a href="/wiki/Language_interpretation" title="Language interpretation">interpretation</a> (for example, information may be encoded into a <a href="/wiki/Sequence" title="Sequence">sequence</a> of <a href="/wiki/Sign_(semiotics)" title="Sign (semiotics)">signs</a>, or transmitted via a <a href="/wiki/Signal" title="Signal">signal</a>). It can also be <a href="/wiki/Encrypted" class="mw-redirect" title="Encrypted">encrypted</a> for safe storage and communication.
</p><p>The uncertainty of an event is measured by its probability of occurrence. Uncertainty is proportional to the negative logarithm of the probability of occurrence. <a href="/wiki/Information_theory" title="Information theory">Information theory</a> takes advantage of this by concluding that more uncertain events require more information to resolve their uncertainty. The <a href="/wiki/Bit" title="Bit">bit</a> is the standard <a href="/wiki/Unit_of_information" class="mw-redirect" title="Unit of information">unit of information</a>. It is 'that which reduces uncertainty by half'.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>6<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Other units such as the <a href="/wiki/Nat_(unit)" title="Nat (unit)">nat</a> may be used. For example, the information encoded in one "fair" coin flip is log<sub>2</sub>(2/1) = 1 bit, and in two fair coin flips is log<sub>2</sub>(4/1) = 2 bits. A 2011 <i><a href="/wiki/Science_(journal)" title="Science (journal)">Science</a></i> article estimates that 97% of technologically stored information was already in digital <a href="/wiki/Bit" title="Bit">bits</a> in 2007 and that the year 2002 was the beginning of the <a href="/wiki/Digital_age" class="mw-redirect" title="Digital age">digital age</a> for information storage (with digital storage capacity bypassing analogue for the first time).<sup id="cite&#95;ref-HilbertLopez2011&#95;7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-HilbertLopez2011-7"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>7<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Etymology_and_history_of_the_concept">Etymology and history of the concept</h2></div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1305433154">.mw-parser-output .ambox{border:1px solid #a2a9b1;border-left:10px solid #36c;background-color:#fbfbfb;box-sizing:border-box}.mw-parser-output </div><span class="hide-when-compact"><i> (<small><a href="/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal" title="Help:Maintenance template removal">Learn how and when to remove this message</a></small>)</i></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>The English word "information" comes from Middle French <i>enformacion/informacion/information</i> 'a criminal investigation' and its etymon, Latin <i>informatiō(n)</i> 'conception, teaching, creation'.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>8<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>In English, "information" is an uncountable <a href="/wiki/Mass_noun" title="Mass noun">mass noun</a>.
</p><p>References on "formation or molding of the mind or character, training, instruction, teaching" date from the 14th century in both English (according to <a href="/wiki/Oxford_English_Dictionary" title="Oxford English Dictionary">Oxford English Dictionary</a>) and other European languages. 
In the transition from Middle Ages to Modernity the use of the concept of information reflected a fundamental turn in epistemological basis – from "giving a (substantial) form to matter" to "communicating something to someone". Peters (1988, pp.&#160;12–13) concludes:
</p>
<dl><dd>Information was readily deployed in empiricist psychology (though it played a less important role than other words such as impression or idea) because it seemed to describe the mechanics of sensation: objects in the world inform the senses. But sensation is entirely different from "form" – the one is sensual, the other intellectual; the one is subjective, the other objective. My sensation of things is fleeting, elusive, and idiosyncratic. For Hume, especially, sensory experience is a swirl of impressions cut off from any sure link to the real world...  In any case, the empiricist problematic was how the mind is informed by sensations of the world. At first informed meant shaped by; later it came to mean received reports from. As its site of action drifted from cosmos to consciousness, the term's sense shifted from unities (Aristotle's forms) to units (of sensation). Information came less and less to refer to internal ordering or formation, since empiricism allowed for no preexisting intellectual forms outside of sensation itself. Instead, information came to refer to the fragmentary, fluctuating, haphazard stuff of sense. Information, like the early modern worldview in general, shifted from a divinely ordered cosmos to a system governed by the motion of corpuscles. Under the tutelage of empiricism, information gradually moved from structure to stuff, from form to substance, from intellectual order to sensory impulses.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>9<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></dd></dl>
<p>In the modern era, the most important influence on the concept of information is derived from the <a href="/wiki/Information_theory" title="Information theory">Information theory</a> developed by <a href="/wiki/Claude_Shannon" title="Claude Shannon">Claude Shannon</a> and others. This theory, however, reflects a fundamental contradiction. Northrup (1993)<sup id="cite&#95;ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>10<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> wrote:
</p>
<dl><dd>Thus, actually two conflicting metaphors are being used: The well-known metaphor of information as a quantity, like water in the water-pipe, is at work, but so is a second metaphor, that of information as a choice, a choice made by :an information provider, and a forced choice made by an :information receiver. Actually, the second metaphor implies that the information sent isn't necessarily equal to the information received, because any choice implies a comparison with a list of possibilities, i.e., a list of possible meanings. Here, meaning is involved, thus spoiling the idea of information as a pure "Ding an sich." Thus, much of the confusion regarding the concept of information seems to be related to the basic confusion of metaphors in Shannon's theory: is information an autonomous quantity, or is information always per SE information to an observer? Actually, I don't think that Shannon himself chose one of the two definitions. Logically speaking, his theory implied information as a subjective phenomenon. But this had so wide-ranging epistemological impacts that Shannon didn't seem to fully realize this logical fact. Consequently, he continued to use metaphors about information as if it were an objective substance. This is the basic, inherent contradiction in Shannon's information theory." (Northrup, 1993, p. 5)</dd></dl>
<p>In their seminal book <i>The Study of Information: Interdisciplinary Messages</i>,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>11<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Almach and Mansfield (1983) collected key views on the interdisciplinary controversy in computer science, artificial intelligence, library and information science, linguistics, psychology, and physics, as well as in the social sciences. Almach (1983,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>12<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> p.&#160;660) himself disagrees with the use of the concept of information in the context of signal transmission, the basic senses of information in his view all referring "to telling something or to the something that is being told. Information is addressed to human minds and is received by human minds." All other senses, including its use with regard to nonhuman organisms as well to society as a whole, are, according to Machlup, metaphoric and, as in the case of cybernetics, anthropomorphic.
</p><p>Hjørland (2007) <sup id="cite&#95;ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>13<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> describes the fundamental difference between objective and subjective views of information and argues that the subjective view has been supported by, among others, Bateson,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>14<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Yovits,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>15<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>16<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Span-Hansen,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>17<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Brier,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>18<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Buckland,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>19<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Goguen,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Goguen&#95;20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Goguen-20"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>20<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> and Hjørland.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>21<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Hjørland provided the following example: 
</p>
<dl><dd>A stone on a field could contain different information for different people (or from one situation to another). It is not possible for information systems to map all the stone's possible information for every individual. Nor is any one mapping the one "true" mapping. But people have different educational backgrounds and play different roles in the division of labor in society. A stone in a field represents typical one kind of information for the geologist, another for the archaeologist. The information from the stone can be mapped into different collective knowledge structures produced by e.g. geology and archaeology. Information can be identified, described, represented in information systems for different domains of knowledge. Of course, there are much uncertainty and many and difficult problems in determining whether a thing is informative or not for a domain. Some domains have high degree of consensus and rather explicit criteria of relevance. Other domains have different, conflicting paradigms, each containing its own more or less implicate view of the informativeness of different kinds of information sources. (Hjørland, 1997, p. 111, emphasis in original).</dd></dl>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Information_theory">Information theory</h2></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Information_theory" title="Information theory">Information theory</a></div>
<p>Information theory is the scientific study of the <a href="/wiki/Quantification_(science)" title="Quantification (science)">quantification</a>, <a href="/wiki/Computer_data_storage" title="Computer data storage">storage</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Telecommunication" class="mw-redirect" title="Telecommunication">communication</a> of information. The field itself was fundamentally established by the work of <a href="/wiki/Claude_Shannon" title="Claude Shannon">Claude Shannon</a> in the 1940s, with earlier contributions by <a href="/wiki/Harry_Nyquist" title="Harry Nyquist">Harry Nyquist</a> and <a href="/wiki/Ralph_Hartley" title="Ralph Hartley">Ralph Hartley</a> in the 1920s.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>22<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>23<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The field is at the intersection of <a href="/wiki/Probability_theory" title="Probability theory">probability theory</a>, <a href="/wiki/Statistics" title="Statistics">statistics</a>, computer science, <a href="/wiki/Statistical_mechanics" title="Statistical mechanics">statistical mechanics</a>, <a href="/wiki/Information_engineering" title="Information engineering">information engineering</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Electrical_engineering" title="Electrical engineering">electrical engineering</a>.
</p><p>A key measure in information theory is <a href="/wiki/Information_entropy" class="mw-redirect" title="Information entropy">entropy</a>. Entropy quantifies the amount of uncertainty involved in the value of a <a href="/wiki/Random_variable" title="Random variable">random variable</a> or the outcome of a <a href="/wiki/Random_process" class="mw-redirect" title="Random process">random process</a>. For example, identifying the outcome of a fair <a href="/wiki/Coin_flip" class="mw-redirect" title="Coin flip">coin flip</a> (with two equally likely outcomes) provides less information (lower entropy) than specifying the outcome from a roll of a <a href="/wiki/Dice" title="Dice">die</a> (with six equally likely outcomes). Some other important measures in information theory are <a href="/wiki/Mutual_information" title="Mutual information">mutual information</a>, channel capacity, <a href="/wiki/Error_exponent" title="Error exponent">error exponents</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Relative_entropy" class="mw-redirect" title="Relative entropy">relative entropy</a>. Important sub-fields of information theory include <a href="/wiki/Source_coding" class="mw-redirect" title="Source coding">source coding</a>, <a href="/wiki/Algorithmic_complexity_theory" class="mw-redirect" title="Algorithmic complexity theory">algorithmic complexity theory</a>, <a href="/wiki/Algorithmic_information_theory" title="Algorithmic information theory">algorithmic information theory</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Information-theoretic_security" title="Information-theoretic security">information-theoretic security</a>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (September 2025)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Applications of fundamental topics of information theory include source coding/<a href="/wiki/Data_compression" title="Data compression">data compression</a> (e.g. for <a href="/wiki/ZIP_files" class="mw-redirect" title="ZIP files">ZIP files</a>), and channel coding/<a href="/wiki/Error_detection_and_correction" title="Error detection and correction">error detection and correction</a> (e.g. for <a href="/wiki/DSL" class="mw-redirect" title="DSL">DSL</a>). Its impact has been crucial to the success of the <a href="/wiki/Voyager_program" title="Voyager program">Voyager</a> missions to deep space, the invention of the <a href="/wiki/Compact_disc" title="Compact disc">compact disc</a>, the feasibility of mobile phones and the development of the Internet. The theory has also found applications in other areas, including <a href="/wiki/Statistical_inference" title="Statistical inference">statistical inference</a>,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>24<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Cryptography" title="Cryptography">cryptography</a>, <a href="/wiki/Neurobiology" class="mw-redirect" title="Neurobiology">neurobiology</a>,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Spikes&#95;25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Spikes-25"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>25<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Perception" title="Perception">perception</a>,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>26<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> linguistics, the evolution<sup id="cite&#95;ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>27<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> and function<sup id="cite&#95;ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>28<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> of molecular codes (<a href="/wiki/Bioinformatics" title="Bioinformatics">bioinformatics</a>), <a href="/wiki/Thermal_physics" title="Thermal physics">thermal physics</a>,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>29<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Quantum_computing" title="Quantum computing">quantum computing</a>, <a href="/wiki/Black_hole_information_paradox" title="Black hole information paradox">black holes</a>, <a href="/wiki/Information_retrieval" title="Information retrieval">information retrieval</a>, <a href="/wiki/Intelligence_(Information_Gathering)" class="mw-redirect" title="Intelligence (Information Gathering)">intelligence gathering</a>, <a href="/wiki/Plagiarism_detection" class="mw-redirect" title="Plagiarism detection">plagiarism detection</a>,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>30<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Pattern_recognition" title="Pattern recognition">pattern recognition</a>, <a href="/wiki/Anomaly_detection" title="Anomaly detection">anomaly detection</a><sup id="cite&#95;ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>31<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> and even art creation.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="As_sensory_input">As sensory input</h2></div>
<p>Often information can be viewed as a type of input to an <a href="/wiki/Organism" title="Organism">organism</a> or <a href="/wiki/System" title="System">system</a>. Inputs are of two kinds. Some inputs are important to the function of the organism (for example, food) or system (<a href="/wiki/Energy" title="Energy">energy</a>) by themselves. In his book <i>Sensory Ecology</i><sup id="cite&#95;ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>32<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> biophysicist <a href="/wiki/David_B._Dusenbery" title="David B. Dusenbery">David B. Dusenbery</a> called these causal inputs. Other inputs (information) are important only because they are associated with causal inputs and can be used to <a href="/wiki/Predict" class="mw-redirect" title="Predict">predict</a> the occurrence of a causal input at a later time (and perhaps another place). Some information is important because of association with other information but eventually there must be a connection to a causal input.
</p><p>In practice, information is usually carried by weak stimuli that must be detected by specialized sensory systems and amplified by energy inputs before they can be functional to the organism or system. For example, light is mainly (but not only, e.g. plants can grow in the direction of the light source) a causal input to plants but for animals it only provides information. The colored light reflected from a flower is too weak for photosynthesis but the visual system of the bee detects it and the bee's nervous system uses the information to guide the bee to the flower, where the bee often finds nectar or pollen, which are causal inputs, a nutritional function.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="As_an_influence_that_leads_to_transformation">As an influence that leads to transformation</h2></div>
<p>Information is any type of pattern that influences the formation or transformation of other patterns.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>33<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>34<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> In this sense, there is no need for a conscious mind to perceive, much less appreciate, the pattern. Consider, for example, <a href="/wiki/DNA" title="DNA">DNA</a>. The sequence of <a href="/wiki/Nucleotide" title="Nucleotide">nucleotides</a> is a pattern that influences the formation and development of an <a href="/wiki/Organism" title="Organism">organism</a> without any need for a conscious mind. One might argue though that for a human to consciously define a pattern, for example a nucleotide, naturally involves conscious information processing. However, the existence of <a href="/wiki/Unicellular" class="mw-redirect" title="Unicellular">unicellular</a> and <a href="/wiki/Multicellular" class="mw-redirect" title="Multicellular">multicellular</a> organisms, with the complex <a href="/wiki/Biochemistry" title="Biochemistry">biochemistry</a> that leads, among other events, to the existence of <a href="/wiki/Enzymes" class="mw-redirect" title="Enzymes">enzymes</a> and polynucleotides that interact maintaining the biological order and participating in the development of multicellular organisms, precedes by millions of years the emergence of human consciousness and the creation of the scientific culture that produced the chemical nomenclature.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Systems_theory" title="Systems theory">Systems theory</a> at times seems to refer to information in this sense, assuming information does not necessarily involve any conscious mind, and patterns circulating (due to <a href="/wiki/Feedback" title="Feedback">feedback</a>) in the system can be called information. In other words, it can be said that information in this sense is something potentially perceived as representation, though not created or presented for that purpose. For example, <a href="/wiki/Gregory_Bateson" title="Gregory Bateson">Gregory Bateson</a> defines "information" as a "difference that makes a difference".<sup id="cite&#95;ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>35<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>If, however, the premise of "influence" implies that information has been perceived by a conscious mind and also interpreted by it, the specific <a href="/wiki/Context_(linguistics)" class="mw-redirect" title="Context (linguistics)">context</a> associated with this interpretation may cause the transformation of the information into <a href="/wiki/Knowledge" title="Knowledge">knowledge</a>. Complex definitions of both "information" and "knowledge" make such semantic and logical analysis difficult, but the condition of "transformation" is an important point in the study of information as it relates to knowledge, especially in the business discipline of <a href="/wiki/Knowledge_management" title="Knowledge management">knowledge management</a>. In this practice, tools and processes are used to assist a <a href="/wiki/Knowledge_worker" title="Knowledge worker">knowledge worker</a> in performing research and making decisions, including steps such as:
</p>
<ul><li>Review information to effectively derive value and meaning</li>
<li>Reference <a href="/wiki/Metadata" title="Metadata">metadata</a> if available</li>
<li>Establish <a href="/wiki/Relevance" title="Relevance">relevant</a> context, often from many possible contexts</li>
<li>Derive new knowledge from the information</li>
<li>Make decisions or recommendations from the resulting knowledge</li></ul>
<p>Stewart (2001) argues that transformation of information into knowledge is critical, lying at the core of value creation and <a href="/wiki/Competitive_advantage" title="Competitive advantage">competitive advantage</a> for the modern enterprise.
</p><p>In a biological framework, Mizraji <sup id="cite&#95;ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>36<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> has described information as an entity emerging from the interaction of patterns with receptor systems (e.g.: in molecular or neural receptors capable of interacting with specific patterns, information emerges from those interactions). In addition, he has incorporated the idea of "information catalysts", structures where emerging information promotes the transition from pattern recognition to goal-directed action (for example, the specific transformation of a substrate into a product by an enzyme, or auditory reception of words and the production of an oral response)
</p><p>The Danish Dictionary of Information Terms<sup id="cite&#95;ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>37<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> argues that information only provides an answer to a posed question. Whether the answer provides knowledge depends on the informed person. So a generalized definition of the concept should be: "Information" = An answer to a specific question".
</p><p>When <a href="/wiki/Marshall_McLuhan" title="Marshall McLuhan">Marshall McLuhan</a> speaks of <a href="/wiki/Media_(communication)" class="mw-redirect" title="Media (communication)">media</a> and their effects on human cultures, he refers to the structure of <a href="/wiki/Cultural_artifact" title="Cultural artifact">artifacts</a> that in turn shape our behaviors and mindsets. Also, <a href="/wiki/Pheromone" title="Pheromone">pheromones</a> are often said to be "information" in this sense.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Technologically_mediated_information">Technologically mediated information</h2></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Information_Age" title="Information Age">Information Age</a></div>
<p>These sections are using measurements of data rather than information, as information cannot be directly measured.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="As_of_2007">As of 2007</h3></div>
<p>It is estimated that the world's technological capacity to store information grew from 2.6 (optimally compressed) <a href="/wiki/Exabytes" class="mw-redirect" title="Exabytes">exabytes</a> in 1986 – which is the informational equivalent to less than one 730-MB <a href="/wiki/CD-ROM" title="CD-ROM">CD-ROM</a> per person (539 MB per person) – to 295 (optimally compressed) <a href="/wiki/Exabytes" class="mw-redirect" title="Exabytes">exabytes</a> in 2007.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-HilbertLopez2011&#95;7-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-HilbertLopez2011-7"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>7<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> This is the informational equivalent of almost 61 <a href="/wiki/CD-ROM" title="CD-ROM">CD-ROM</a> per person in 2007.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Hilbertvideo2011&#95;5-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hilbertvideo2011-5"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>5<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>The world's combined technological capacity to receive information through one-way <a href="/wiki/Broadcast" class="mw-redirect" title="Broadcast">broadcast</a> networks was the informational equivalent of 174 <a href="/wiki/Newspaper" title="Newspaper">newspapers</a> per person per day in 2007.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-HilbertLopez2011&#95;7-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-HilbertLopez2011-7"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>7<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>The world's combined effective capacity to exchange information through two-way <a href="/wiki/Telecommunication" class="mw-redirect" title="Telecommunication">telecommunication</a> networks was the informational equivalent of 6 newspapers per person per day in 2007.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Hilbertvideo2011&#95;5-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hilbertvideo2011-5"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>5<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>As of 2007, an estimated 90% of all new information is digital, mostly stored on hard drives.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>38<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="As_of_2020">As of 2020</h3></div>
<p>The total amount of data created, captured, copied, and consumed globally is forecast to increase rapidly, reaching 64.2 zettabytes in 2020. Over the next five years up to 2025, global data creation is projected to grow to more than 180 zettabytes.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>39<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="As_records">As records</h2></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1333133064" /><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1246091330" /><table class="sidebar sidebar-collapse nomobile nowraplinks hlist" role="navigation"><tbody><tr><td class="sidebar-pretitle">Part of a series on</td></tr><tr><th class="sidebar-title-with-pretitle"><a href="/wiki/Library_and_information_science" title="Library and information science">Library and information science</a></th></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-image"><span class="mw-image-border" typeof="mw:File/Frameless"><a href="/wiki/File:LIS_SidebarPicture.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/LIS_SidebarPicture.png/250px-LIS_SidebarPicture.png" decoding="async" width="250" height="162" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/LIS_SidebarPicture.png/500px-LIS_SidebarPicture.png 1.5x" data-file-width="2544" data-file-height="1644" /></a></span></td></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-above" style="padding-bottom:0.35em;">

<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:WikiProject_Libraries" title="Wikipedia:WikiProject Libraries">WikiProject</a></li>
<li><span class="nowrap"><span class="noviewer" typeof="mw:File"><span title="Category"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/96/Symbol_category_class.svg/20px-Symbol_category_class.svg.png" decoding="async" width="16" height="16" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/96/Symbol_category_class.svg/40px-Symbol_category_class.svg.png 1.5x" data-file-width="180" data-file-height="185" /></span></span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Category:Library_and_information_science" title="Category:Library and information science">Category</a></span></li></ul></td></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1333133064" /><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1239400231" /><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:LibraryandInformation-TopicSidebar" title="Template:LibraryandInformation-TopicSidebar"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:LibraryandInformation-TopicSidebar" title="Template talk:LibraryandInformation-TopicSidebar"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a href="/wiki/Special:EditPage/Template:LibraryandInformation-TopicSidebar" title="Special:EditPage/Template:LibraryandInformation-TopicSidebar"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>Records are specialized forms of information. Essentially, records are information produced consciously or as by-products of business activities or transactions and retained because of their value. Primarily, their value is as evidence of the activities of the organization but they may also be retained for their informational value. Sound <a href="/wiki/Records_management" title="Records management">records management</a> ensures that the integrity of records is preserved for as long as they are required.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (May 2024)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>The international standard on records management, ISO 15489, defines records as "information created, received, and maintained as evidence and information by an organization or person, in pursuance of legal obligations or in the transaction of business".<sup id="cite&#95;ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>40<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The International Committee on Archives (ICA) Committee on electronic records defined a record as, "recorded information produced or received in the initiation, conduct or completion of an institutional or individual activity and that comprises content, context and structure sufficient to provide evidence of the activity".<sup id="cite&#95;ref-ica&#95;41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ica-41"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>41<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Records may be maintained to retain <a href="/wiki/Corporate_memory" class="mw-redirect" title="Corporate memory">corporate memory</a> of the organization or to meet legal, fiscal or accountability requirements imposed on the organization. Willis expressed the view that sound management of business records and information delivered "...six key requirements for good <a href="/wiki/Corporate_governance" title="Corporate governance">corporate governance</a>...transparency; accountability; due process; compliance; meeting statutory and common law requirements; and security of personal and corporate information."<sup id="cite&#95;ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>42<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Semiotics">Semiotics</h2></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Michael_Buckland" title="Michael Buckland">Michael Buckland</a> has classified "information" in terms of its uses: "information as process", "information as knowledge", and "information as thing".<sup id="cite&#95;ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>43<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Paul_Beynon-Davies" title="Paul Beynon-Davies">Beynon-Davies</a><sup id="cite&#95;ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>44<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>45<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> explains the multi-faceted concept of information in terms of signs and signal-sign systems. Signs themselves can be considered in terms of four inter-dependent levels, layers or branches of <a href="/wiki/Semiotics" title="Semiotics">semiotics</a>: pragmatics, semantics, syntax, and empirics. These four layers serve to connect the social world on the one hand with the physical or technical world on the other.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Pragmatics" title="Pragmatics">Pragmatics</a> is concerned with the purpose of communication. Pragmatics links the issue of signs with the context within which signs are used. The focus of pragmatics is on the intentions of living agents underlying communicative behaviour. In other words, pragmatics link language to action.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Semantics" title="Semantics">Semantics</a> is concerned with the meaning of a message conveyed in a communicative act. Semantics considers the content of communication. Semantics is the study of the meaning of signs – the association between signs and behaviour. Semantics can be considered as the study of the link between symbols and their referents or concepts – particularly the way that signs relate to human behavior.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Syntax" title="Syntax">Syntax</a> is concerned with the formalism used to represent a message. Syntax as an area studies the form of communication in terms of the logic and grammar of sign systems. Syntax is devoted to the study of the form rather than the content of signs and sign systems.
</p><p>Nielsen (2008) discusses the relationship between semiotics and information in relation to dictionaries. He introduces the concept of <a href="/wiki/Lexicographic_information_cost" title="Lexicographic information cost">lexicographic information costs</a> and refers to the effort a user of a dictionary must make to first find, and then understand data so that they can generate information.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>46<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Communication normally exists within the context of some social situation. The social situation sets the context for the intentions conveyed (pragmatics) and the form of communication. In a communicative situation intentions are expressed through messages that comprise collections of inter-related signs taken from a language mutually understood by the agents involved in the communication. Mutual understanding implies that agents involved understand the chosen language in terms of its agreed syntax and semantics. The sender codes the message in the language and sends the message as signals along some communication channel (empirics). The chosen communication channel has inherent properties that determine outcomes such as the speed at which communication can take place, and over what distance.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Physics_and_determinacy">Physics and determinacy</h2></div>
<p>The existence of information about a <a href="/wiki/Closed_system" title="Closed system">closed system</a> is a major concept in both <a href="/wiki/Classical_physics" title="Classical physics">classical physics</a> and <a href="/wiki/Quantum_mechanics" title="Quantum mechanics">quantum mechanics</a>, encompassing the ability, real or theoretical, of an agent to predict the future state of a system based on knowledge gathered during its past and present. <a href="/wiki/Determinism" title="Determinism">Determinism</a> is a philosophical theory holding that causal determination can predict all future events,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>47<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> positing a fully predictable <a href="/wiki/Universe" title="Universe">universe</a> described by classical physicist <a href="/wiki/Pierre-Simon_Laplace" title="Pierre-Simon Laplace">Pierre-Simon Laplace</a> as "<a href="/wiki/Laplace%27s_demon" title="Laplace&#39;s demon">the effect of its past and the cause of its future</a>".<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Truscott&#95;48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Truscott-48"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>48<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Quantum physics instead encodes information as a <a href="/wiki/Wave_function" title="Wave function">wave function</a>, a mathematical description of a system from which the probabilities of <a href="/wiki/Measurement_in_quantum_mechanics" title="Measurement in quantum mechanics">measurement outcomes</a> can be computed. A fundamental feature of quantum theory is that the predictions it makes are <a href="/wiki/Probability" title="Probability">probabilistic</a>. Prior to the publication of <a href="/wiki/Bell%27s_theorem" title="Bell&#39;s theorem">Bell's theorem</a>, determinists reconciled with this behavior using <a href="/wiki/Hidden_variable_theories" class="mw-redirect" title="Hidden variable theories">hidden variable theories</a>, which argued that the information necessary to predict the future of a function <i>must</i> exist, even if it is not accessible for humans, a view expressed by <a href="/wiki/Albert_Einstein" title="Albert Einstein">Albert Einstein</a> with the assertion that "<a href="/wiki/God_does_not_play_dice" class="mw-redirect" title="God does not play dice">God does not play dice</a>".<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Einstein&#95;letter,&#95;4&#95;Dec&#95;1926&#95;49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Einstein_letter,_4_Dec_1926-49"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>49<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Modern <a href="/wiki/Astronomy" title="Astronomy">astronomy</a> cites the mechanical sense of information in the <a href="/wiki/Black_hole_information_paradox" title="Black hole information paradox">black hole information paradox</a>, positing that, because the complete evaporation of a <a href="/wiki/Black_hole" title="Black hole">black hole</a> into <a href="/wiki/Hawking_radiation" title="Hawking radiation">Hawking radiation</a> leaves nothing except an expanding cloud of <a href="/wiki/Homogeneous" class="mw-redirect" title="Homogeneous">homogeneous</a> particles, this results in the irrecoverability of any information about the matter to have originally crossed the <a href="/wiki/Event_horizon" title="Event horizon">event horizon</a>, violating both classical and quantum assertions against the ability to destroy information.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-SH-2006&#95;50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SH-2006-50"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>50<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-NYT-20130812&#95;51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NYT-20130812-51"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>51<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="The_application_of_information_study">The application of information study</h2></div>
<p>The information cycle (addressed as a whole or in its distinct components) is of great concern to <a href="/wiki/Information_technology" title="Information technology">information technology</a>, <a href="/wiki/Information_systems" class="mw-redirect" title="Information systems">information systems</a>, as well as <a href="/wiki/Information_science" title="Information science">information science</a>. These fields deal with those processes and techniques pertaining to information capture (through <a href="/wiki/Sensors" class="mw-redirect" title="Sensors">sensors</a>) and generation (through <a href="/wiki/Computation" title="Computation">computation</a>, <a href="/wiki/Formulation" title="Formulation">formulation</a> or composition), <a href="/wiki/Data_processing" title="Data processing">processing</a> (including encoding, encryption, compression, packaging), <a href="/wiki/Data_transmission" class="mw-redirect" title="Data transmission">transmission</a> (including all <a href="/wiki/Telecommunication" class="mw-redirect" title="Telecommunication">telecommunication</a> methods), presentation (including <a href="/wiki/Information_visualization" class="mw-redirect" title="Information visualization">visualization</a> / <a href="/wiki/Display_device" title="Display device">display</a> methods), <a href="/wiki/Digital_Storage" class="mw-redirect" title="Digital Storage">storage</a> (such as magnetic or optical, including <a href="/wiki/Holographic_data_storage" title="Holographic data storage">holographic methods</a>), etc.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Information_visualization" class="mw-redirect" title="Information visualization">Information visualization</a> (shortened as InfoVis) depends on the computation and digital representation of data, and assists users in <a href="/wiki/Pattern_recognition" title="Pattern recognition">pattern recognition</a> and <a href="/wiki/Anomaly_detection" title="Anomaly detection">anomaly detection</a>.
</p>
<ul class="gallery mw-gallery-traditional">
		<li class="gallerybox" style="width: 155px">
			<div class="thumb" style="width: 150px; height: 150px;"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Internet_map_1024.jpg" class="mw-file-description" title="Partial map of the Internet, with nodes representing IP addresses"><img alt="Partial map of the Internet, with nodes representing IP addresses" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d2/Internet_map_1024.jpg/120px-Internet_map_1024.jpg" decoding="async" width="120" height="120" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d2/Internet_map_1024.jpg/250px-Internet_map_1024.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="1280" data-file-height="1280" /></a></span></div>
			<div class="gallerytext">Partial map of the Internet, with nodes representing IP addresses</div>
		</li>
		<li class="gallerybox" style="width: 155px">
			<div class="thumb" style="width: 150px; height: 150px;"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Structure_of_the_Universe.jpg" class="mw-file-description" title="Galactic (including dark) matter distribution in a cubic section of the Universe"><img alt="Galactic (including dark) matter distribution in a cubic section of the Universe" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/Structure_of_the_Universe.jpg/120px-Structure_of_the_Universe.jpg" decoding="async" width="120" height="98" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/Structure_of_the_Universe.jpg/250px-Structure_of_the_Universe.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="2682" data-file-height="2196" /></a></span></div>
			<div class="gallerytext">Galactic (including dark) matter distribution in a cubic section of the Universe</div>
		</li>
		<li class="gallerybox" style="width: 155px">
			<div class="thumb" style="width: 150px; height: 150px;"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Attractor_Poisson_Saturne.jpg" class="mw-file-description" title="Visual representation of a strange attractor, with converted data of its fractal structure"><img alt="Visual representation of a strange attractor, with converted data of its fractal structure" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4a/Attractor_Poisson_Saturne.jpg/120px-Attractor_Poisson_Saturne.jpg" decoding="async" width="120" height="90" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4a/Attractor_Poisson_Saturne.jpg/250px-Attractor_Poisson_Saturne.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="480" /></a></span></div>
			<div class="gallerytext">Visual representation of a strange attractor, with converted data of its fractal structure</div>
		</li>
</ul>
<p><a href="/wiki/Information_security" title="Information security">Information security</a> (shortened as InfoSec) is the ongoing process of exercising due diligence to protect information, and information systems, from unauthorized access, use, disclosure, destruction, modification, disruption or distribution, through algorithms and procedures focused on monitoring and detection, as well as incident response and repair.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Information_analysis" class="mw-redirect" title="Information analysis">Information analysis</a> is the process of inspecting, transforming, and modeling information, by converting raw data into actionable knowledge, in support of the decision-making process.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Information_Quality_(InfoQ)" class="mw-redirect" title="Information Quality (InfoQ)">Information quality</a> (shortened as InfoQ) is the potential of a dataset to achieve a specific (scientific or practical) goal using a given empirical analysis method.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Infocommunications" title="Infocommunications">Information communication</a> represents the convergence of informatics, telecommunication and audio-visual media &amp; content.
</p>
